  \node[decision, below=1.0cm of fw, text width=2.0cm] (edit) {Client\\reslices it\\herself?};
  \node[stage, right=1.9cm of edit, text width=2.0cm, fill=aeBg] (sheet) {Spreadsheet\\(Excel /\\Google)};
  \node[decision, below=1.0cm of edit, text width=2.0cm] (change) {Must it\\ever change?};
  \node[stage, right=1.9cm of change, text width=2.0cm, fill=aeBg] (pdf) {PDF\\snapshot};
  \node[stage, below=1.0cm of change, text width=2.4cm] (portal) {\textbf{Offline portal}\\\scriptsize this chapter};
  \draw[flow] (fw) -- node[annot,above]{no} (live);
  \draw[flow] (live) -- node[annot,above]{yes} (host);
  \draw[flow] (fw) -- node[annot,left]{yes} (edit);
  \draw[flow] (live) -- node[annot,right]{no}
      ($(live.south)+(0,-0.15)$) -| (edit.east);
  \draw[flow] (edit) -- node[annot,above]{yes} (sheet);
  \draw[flow] (edit) -- node[annot,left]{no} (change);
  \draw[flow] (change) -- node[annot,above]{no} (pdf);
  \draw[flow] (change) -- node[annot,left]{yes} (portal);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\caption[Choosing a delivery format]{The same choice Table~\ref{tab:formats} scores, read as a decision path. Follow the questions and each answer routes to one format: a live refresh behind no firewall wants a hosted dashboard; hands-on client reslicing wants a spreadsheet; a fixed record that never changes wants a PDF; and the deliverable that must clear a firewall yet still update itself lands on the offline portal. The portal is the only leaf reached by insisting on both a firewall-safe format and one that changes with the data.}
\label{fig:ch12-viz-choose}
\end{figure*}

\section{Versioning the artifacts you hand over}\label{sec:versioning}
A portal the client owns raises a question a hosted dashboard never has to answer: which version is this? A dashboard shows whatever the server holds right now, so there is only ever one. A folder the client can copy, email, and archive multiplies, and six months later a board member is looking at a completion rate on a laptop and no one can say whether it is the number the committee approved in the spring or a later revision. The fix is to stamp every artifact you hand over, so that any copy carries its own provenance and can never be mistaken for another.

Stamp three things, and stamp them where they cannot be lost.\marginnote{Borrow the semantic-versioning habit from software: bump the middle number when the numbers change, the last when only wording or layout does. A board that saw \enquote{v1.2} and hears \enquote{v1.3.1} landed knows nothing material moved.} First, put a visible version and date on the landing page itself, so a reader sees at a glance which cut they are holding. Second, name the folder with the same stamp, so a copy on a shared drive is identified before anyone opens it. Third, and most important, record the commit or tag of the code that produced the folder, so \enquote{which numbers did the board see} resolves to \enquote{run the pipeline at this commit and watch them reappear.} The first two are for humans skimming; the third is the one an auditor or a successor evaluator actually needs. Table~\ref{tab:stamps} lines the three up side by side, and reading down it you can see that each stamp answers a different reader's question.

\begin{table*}[htbp]
\centering
\footnotesize
\caption[What to stamp on a delivered portal]{The three stamps that keep delivered copies from being confused, and the question each one answers. The visible stamp is for a board member skimming; the folder name is for a colleague browsing a shared drive; the code tag is for the auditor who has to reproduce the exact numbers.}
\label{tab:stamps}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\hline
Stamp & Where it lives & Question it answers \\
\hline
Version \& date & on \texttt{index.html} & \enquote{which cut is this?} \\
Folder name     & \texttt{site-portal-2026Q1/} & \enquote{which copy is on the drive?} \\
Code tag        & footer \& \texttt{README} & \enquote{how do I rebuild it?} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table*}

The mechanics are cheap because you already own the pieces. The date is a system macro, so the report stamps itself at build time rather than relying on anyone to remember; the same \texttt{\$S\_DATE} that dated your logs in Chapter~\ref{ch:workshop} dates the portal.\marginnote{A tag is not a comment. Writing \enquote{v1.3} in the footer by hand is a promise you can break; \texttt{git tag v1.3} on the commit that built the folder is a promise the version-control system keeps. The first tells the reader a story about the version; the second lets an auditor check it.} The version is a string you set once per delivery in the control file. The code tag is a one-line \texttt{git} operation on the commit that produced the build; \texttt{commit} records a snapshot of the code, \texttt{tag} pins a name to that snapshot, and \texttt{checkout} returns to it later, which is all the \texttt{git} this chapter needs; Chapter~\ref{ch:tools} returns to tags when a package release has to stay reinstallable. Weave the three into the build so they are produced by the run, not added by hand afterward:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* set once per delivery, at the top of the build:
global version "v1.3"

* stamp the report footer at build time:
webdoc put ---
webdoc put Version $version, built $S_DATE.
webdoc put Rebuild: git checkout $version, then
webdoc put run 600_report.do.
\end{verbatim}
}
Because the stamp is produced by the same run that produced the numbers, it cannot drift from them, which is the same structural guarantee that made the \texttt{webdoc} report trustworthy in the first place. A board member holding a laptop reads the footer and knows the cut; a colleague on the shared drive reads the folder name and knows the copy; an auditor reads the tag and rebuilds the exact numbers. That closes the last gap in owning the evidence: not just that the client has the folder, but that everyone can tell, forever, which folder it is.

Whether a discipline of this kind actually changes behavior is an empirical question, and the evidence says it does when the requirement is enforced rather than merely encouraged.\marginnote{\textcite{stodden2018reproducibility} studied journals that adopted code-and-data sharing policies: the policies raised the share of reproducible results, but only where the journal checked compliance rather than trusting authors to self-report.} The lesson for an embedded evaluator is that a stamping habit works the same way. A version typed by hand into a footer is a policy no one enforces; a \texttt{git} tag on the commit that built the folder is a check the version-control system runs for you, which is why the tag, not the typed string, is the promise worth keeping.

\subsection{Stamp the code commit and the data vintage, not just the version}
The footer stamp so far records which version the reader holds, but a board that has to defend a number needs two more facts the version string alone does not carry: which code produced it and which data went in. \textcite{blischak2016git} introduce version control to working analysts by making one point an evaluator should borrow: a commit hash is a fingerprint of the exact state of the code, so recording it turns \enquote{roughly this analysis} into \enquote{precisely this analysis, byte for byte.} Stamp the short commit hash into the footer, alongside the human-readable tag, and a successor can check out that one commit and watch the same numbers reappear. The tag is the label a board remembers; the hash is the address an auditor resolves.

The data vintage is the second fact, and it is the one evaluators forget most often. A portal built at the same commit against a refreshed extract produces different numbers, so the code hash alone does not pin the result. Record the date the source data was pulled and, where the source gives one, its own release version, so the footer answers not just \enquote{which code} but \enquote{which code against which data.} \textcite{wilkinson2016fair} set out the principle that scholarly data should be findable and its provenance traceable, and a portal footer is where an evaluator makes that principle small and concrete: a board member who reads \enquote{built from the County Health Rankings 2025 release, pulled 12 March} can find the exact extract behind the chart, and a successor can pull the same vintage rather than a newer one that quietly moved the numbers. With both facts in the footer, code hash and data vintage, a delivered folder describes itself.

The easiest way to see what the standard asks is to read one full stamp as a single sentence. A self-describing footer reads roughly: \enquote{Version 1.3, built 6 July 2026 from commit \texttt{a1b9c02}, against the County Health Rankings 2025 release pulled 12 March 2026; rebuild with \texttt{git checkout v1.3} then \texttt{run 600\_report.do}.} Every clause in that sentence answers a question a board or an auditor will eventually ask: the version and date say which cut, the commit says which code, the release and pull date say which data, and the rebuild line says how to reproduce it. An evaluator who writes that one sentence into the footer at build time has met the traceability half of \textcite{wilkinson2016fair} for the modest scope of a single report, without any of the infrastructure a full data repository would demand. The point is not to satisfy a standard for its own sake but to make the folder answer for itself, so that a colleague who finds it on a shared drive three years on needs nothing but the footer to know what they are holding and how to rebuild it. Figure~\ref{fig:ch12-viz-footer} takes that one sentence apart clause by clause, so it is plain which reader each clause serves.

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=0.35cm]
  \node[stage, text width=11.5cm, minimum height=0.9cm, align=left, font=\sffamily\scriptsize] (bar)
    {\texttt{Version 1.3, built 6 Jul 2026}~$\mid$~\texttt{commit a1b9c02}~$\mid$~\texttt{CHR 2025, pulled 12 Mar}~$\mid$~\texttt{git checkout v1.3; run 600\_report.do}};
  \node[substep, below=1.1cm of bar.west, anchor=north west, text width=2.5cm] (n1)
    {\textbf{version + date}\\\scriptsize which cut?};
  \node[substep, right=0.35cm of n1, text width=2.5cm] (n2)
    {\textbf{commit hash}\\\scriptsize which code?};
  \node[substep, right=0.35cm of n2, text width=2.5cm] (n3)
    {\textbf{data vintage}\\\scriptsize which data?};
  \node[substep, right=0.35cm of n3, text width=2.7cm] (n4)
    {\textbf{rebuild line}\\\scriptsize how to reproduce?};
  \draw[flow] (n1.north) -- ($(bar.south west)+(1.3,0)$);
  \draw[flow] (n2.north) -- ($(bar.south west)+(4.6,0)$);
  \draw[flow] (n3.north) -- ($(bar.south west)+(7.6,0)$);
  \draw[flow] (n4.north) -- ($(bar.south west)+(10.4,0)$);
  \node[annot, below=0.3cm of n1, text width=2.5cm] {board member skims};
  \node[annot, below=0.3cm of n2, text width=2.5cm] {auditor resolves};
  \node[annot, below=0.3cm of n3, text width=2.5cm] {successor pulls same};
  \node[annot, below=0.3cm of n4, text width=2.7cm] {anyone rebuilds};
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\caption[Anatomy of a self-describing footer]{The self-describing footer, read clause by clause. The top bar is the whole stamp as it appears on the page; each box below names one clause, the question it answers, and the reader it serves. The version and date tell a board member which cut they hold, the commit hash gives an auditor the exact code, the data vintage lets a successor pull the same extract rather than a newer one that quietly moved the numbers, and the rebuild line lets anyone regenerate the folder. The stamp is produced by the same run that produced the numbers, so no clause can drift from what it describes.}
\label{fig:ch12-viz-footer}
\end{figure*}

The caveat is the same one the equity section named, and it belongs in the footer's honesty too. A stamped static portal tells a reader exactly which cut they hold, but it cannot answer a question about a number the cut does not contain, because there is no live query behind it. A board member who wants this quarter's figure re-sliced by a group the report did not break out is holding a snapshot, not a database, and the footer should not pretend otherwise. Naming the vintage keeps that limit honest: the stamp says \enquote{these are the numbers as of this pull,} which is a promise a static folder can keep, rather than \enquote{ask this folder anything,} which it cannot.

\subsection{The release checklist}\label{ch12-sc-release}
The stamps travel on the artifact; a checklist governs the moment it leaves your hands. A portal ships when someone decides it ships, and the decision goes better read off a short list than made from memory late on a delivery Friday. This chapter's instance of the working-file pattern from Chapter~\ref{ch:workshop} is a release checklist, one plain-text file per delivery, five lines long: the stamp is checked, meaning the footer version, the folder name, and the \texttt{git} tag all agree; every link on the landing page has been clicked and opens; the data vintage named in the footer matches the extract that actually fed the build; the sign-offs are collected; and an archive copy of the shipped folder is stored somewhere the team does not work day to day, so the delivered bytes survive even after the working copy is edited.\marginnote{Date each checklist line as it passes and the checklist doubles as the release record when someone asks, a year later, who approved the spring cut.}

The sign-off list is deliberately short, and keeping it short is the craft. Three names cover a portal release: the analyst who built it confirms the build ran clean from the top; the evaluator who owns the narrative confirms the prose says only what the numbers support; and the program director whose name the board attaches to the figures confirms they are willing to be asked about any number on the page. Each signer answers one question no one else can answer. A fourth or fifth reviewer does not add a fourth question; they add a queue, and portals that wait in queues ship stale, which is its own kind of error in a deliverable whose value is being current. If a stakeholder wants visibility without holding the release, send them the shipped folder the same hour it ships rather than a draft the week before.

\section{The whole suite in one folder}\label{sec:capstone}
Everything in this book that touched the web has been building toward one deliverable, and this section assembles it. The tools introduced across the visualization chapters are not isolated commands; they are a pipeline, and each owns one link in it. The data comes in through \texttt{webapi}\index{webapi@\texttt{webapi}} from a public API (Chapter~\ref{ch:apis}) or through \texttt{googlesheets}\index{googlesheets@\texttt{googlesheets}} from a live team sheet (Chapter~\ref{ch:sheets}). It becomes charts two ways, because the delivery target decides which: \texttt{googlechart}\index{googlechart@\texttt{googlechart}} for an online interactive page with filter dropdowns, and \texttt{sparkta2}\index{sparkta2@\texttt{sparkta2}} for an offline map that renders without a network, including the county and school-district geography that the online engine cannot draw (both Chapter~\ref{ch:interactive}). The interactive drill-down is a \texttt{statashiny} dashboard; when the committee wants headline tiles against a benchmark instead, a \texttt{dashboardbuilder} page (Section~\ref{sec:dashboardbuilder}) fills the same slot with the same one-line embed. And \texttt{webdoc2} wraps all of it, narrative and charts and dashboard, into one \texttt{index.html}: the data goes in at one end, and a folder the client owns comes out the other.

The reason to see the whole chain at once, rather than each tool in its own chapter, is that the chain is the product. A reader who has met the tools separately can still miss that they compose, and composition, not any single tool, turns a pile of HTML files into a portal. Figure~\ref{fig:suiteflow} draws the pipeline, and the do-file sketch that follows walks the same arrows in code.

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=0.6cm and 1.1cm]
  \node[stage, text width=2.5cm] (ingest) {\textbf{Ingest}\\\scriptsize\texttt{webapi}\\\scriptsize\texttt{googlesheets}\\\scriptsize\itshape Ch\,3 / Ch\,11};
  \node[substep, right=of ingest, text width=2.5cm] (online)
    {\texttt{googlechart}\\\scriptsize online chart\\\scriptsize\itshape Ch\,10};
  \node[substep, below=0.5cm of online, text width=2.5cm] (offline)
    {\texttt{sparkta2}\\\scriptsize offline map\\\scriptsize\itshape Ch\,10};
  \node[substep, above=0.5cm of online, text width=2.5cm] (dash)
    {\texttt{statashiny}\\\scriptsize dashboard\\\scriptsize\itshape Ch\,12};
  \node[stage, right=of online, text width=2.5cm, fill=aeBg] (wrap)
    {\textbf{Wrap}\\\scriptsize\texttt{webdoc2}\\\scriptsize\itshape Ch\,12};
  \node[stage, right=of wrap, text width=2.4cm] (out)
    {\sffamily\textbf{index.html}\\\scriptsize one folder,\\\scriptsize opens offline};
  \draw[flow] (ingest.east) -- (online.west);
  \draw[flow] (ingest.east) to[out=20,in=180] (dash.west);
  \draw[flow] (ingest.east) to[out=-20,in=180] (offline.west);
  \draw[flow] (dash.east) to[out=0,in=160] (wrap.north west);
  \draw[flow] (online.east) -- (wrap.west);
  \draw[flow] (offline.east) to[out=0,in=200] (wrap.south west);
  \draw[flow] (wrap.east) -- (out.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[The suite pipeline, end to end]{The suite as one pipeline. \texttt{webapi} or \texttt{googlesheets} ingests the data; \texttt{googlechart} renders an online interactive chart, \texttt{sparkta2} an offline map, and \texttt{statashiny} an interactive dashboard; \texttt{webdoc2} wraps all three into one \texttt{index.html} that opens offline from a single folder. Each box names the chapter that teaches it. The companion \texttt{ch12\_portal.do} walks these same arrows in code.}
\label{fig:suiteflow}
\end{figure*}

The orchestration is one do-file, shown next as a display-only sketch because its middle stages need installed packages and OAuth credentials this chapter does not assume. The sketch below is display-only; it is the pipeline of Figure~\ref{fig:suiteflow} written as the commands you would actually run, one stage at a time. It begins by pulling data with \texttt{webapi}, the no-key JSON route of Chapter~\ref{ch:apis}:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* 1. INGEST: public JSON in one line, no key
webapi get using ///
    "https://data.cdc.gov/resource/bi63-dtpu.json", ///
    params("year=2017|cause_name=All causes") clear
\end{verbatim}
}
With data in memory, the two chart tools write their HTML into the portal's \texttt{charts/} folder (the companion \texttt{ch12\_portal.do} carries the runnable version of this sketch, folder setup included). \texttt{googlechart} draws the online state choropleth; \texttt{sparkta2} draws the offline county map that the online engine cannot. The \texttt{sparkta2} line is commented because its source is still forthcoming (Chapter~\ref{ch:interactive}), so the sketch shows the intended call rather than promising an install:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* 2a. CHART online: googlechart interactive page
googlechart uninsured, name(geo_code) type(geo) ///
    georegion("US") georesolution("us-states") ///
    scheme(blues) download datatable ///
    title("Uninsured rate by state, 2025") ///
    noopen export("$charts/geo_uninsured.html")

* 2b. CHART offline: sparkta2 sub-state map (sketch)
* sparkta2 completed, name(county) ///
*     type(choropleth) geo(tx-counties) ///
*     export("$charts/county_map.html")
\end{verbatim}
}
Next the interactive dashboard, built with the init, add, build workflow from earlier in this chapter, exported into the same \texttt{charts/} folder:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* 3. INTERACTIVE: statashiny drill-down dashboard
statashiny, title("Site Outcomes") replace
statashiny cards, output(val) ///
    label("Completion") suffix("%")
statashiny tbl, output(table) label("By site")
statashiny cut, input(num) ///
    val(50) min(0) max(100) step(5)
statashiny, build export("$charts/dashboard.html")
\end{verbatim}
}
Now the wrap. \texttt{webdoc2} writes \texttt{index.html} with the narrative, embeds the dashboard and the online chart as live panels with \texttt{wdiframe}, drops the offline map with \texttt{wdimg}, and stamps the version and date into the footer at build time so it cannot drift from the numbers (Section~\ref{sec:versioning}):
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* 4. WRAP: webdoc2 assembles the one page
wdinit "$portal/index", replace
wputh1 Q3 Site Outcomes
wput   Rebuilt from the analysis on $S_DATE.
wputh2 Interactive dashboard
wdiframe "charts/dashboard.html", height(640)
wputh2 Uninsured by state
wdiframe "charts/geo_uninsured.html", height(600)
wputh2 Completion by county (offline map)
wdimg  "charts/county_map.png", ///
    caption("Offline county choropleth")
wput ---
wput Version $version, built $S_DATE.
webdoc close
\end{verbatim}
}
The four stages fit in one do-file, and the folder they produce is already a website, so the optional last step is to hand the client a link: push the folder to a repository and turn on GitHub Pages\marginnote{Turning on GitHub Pages is one repository setting: Settings, then Pages, then pick the branch and folder to serve.}, which is how both the \texttt{statashiny} and \texttt{webdoc2} example sites are hosted, at no cost and with no server to keep alive.

The last step before the folder goes anywhere is a rebuild rehearsal. Clone the repository into a fresh directory, on a machine that is not the build machine where you can manage it, run the build do-file from the top, and then do what the steering-committee member will do: double-click \texttt{index.html} and click every link and control once. The rehearsal catches a specific family of failures that a build on your own machine never surfaces: the absolute path to \texttt{charts/} that resolves only under your username, the county map the do-file reads from a folder outside the portal, the \texttt{wdiframe} panel that renders blank under \texttt{file://} because the dashboard's data was never inlined, the community-contributed package the build silently assumed was installed. Every failure in that family is invisible in the environment where the portal was built and near-certain in the environment where it will be opened. The rehearsal costs ten minutes; learning about the blank dashboard from a board member's email costs a week of credibility. Give the rehearsal its own dated line on the release checklist of Section~\ref{ch12-sc-release}, so that shipped always means rehearsed.

One further step takes the assembly, though not the shipping, off your calendar. The build itself, ingest through wrap, runs in batch exactly the way Chapter~\ref{ch:surveys} scheduled its survey pulls: one \texttt{cron} or Task Scheduler line runs the pipeline do-file on the data source's release date, writes the rebuilt folder to a staging location, and leaves a dated log. Resist automating the last mile. The release checklist and its three sign-offs exist because a person, not a scheduler, decides the portal is safe to send, and a pipeline that publishes straight to the client would also auto-publish the one quarter the source quietly rewrote its own history and every rate shifted. Automate up to the staging folder and the quarterly delivery becomes an hour of review instead of a day of assembly, with the judgment kept exactly where it belongs, at the moment of release.

The figures below are real \texttt{googlechart} output from this pipeline on County Health Rankings data, the actual pages a reader opens from the \texttt{charts/} folder.

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
googlechart uninsured, name(geo_code) ///
    type(geo) georegion("US") ///
    georesolution("us-states") ///
    scheme(blues)
\end{verbatim}
}
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{ch10_gc_geo.png}
\caption[The online chart the pipeline embeds]{The \texttt{googlechart} state choropleth from stage 2a of the pipeline, real output on County Health Rankings uninsured rates. In the portal this page is embedded live by \texttt{wdiframe}, so the reader hovers a state and reads its rate without leaving the report.}
\label{fig:capgeo}
\end{figure*}

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
googlechart, type(table) tablesearch ///
    tableheadersticky ///
    tooltipvars(state uninsured ///
        median_income premature_death)
\end{verbatim}
}
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{ch10_gc_table.png}
\caption[The searchable table the dashboard mirrors]{A \texttt{googlechart} searchable table on the same data: a reader types a state and the rows filter live in the browser. The \texttt{statashiny} dashboard of stage~3 offers the same interaction offline, and \texttt{webdoc2} embeds one of them into the portal by \texttt{wdiframe}.}
\label{fig:captable}
\end{figure*}

\begin{appliedexample}[One evaluation, one folder]\label{ae:ch12-onefolder}
\textbf{Scenario.} A client wants one offline folder to share with their steering committee: a narrative report, an interactive dashboard of site outcomes, an online chart, and an offline county map, and they need to know six months from now exactly which cut the committee approved.

\medskip
\textbf{The build, end to end.} Scaffold the folder following the \texttt{projectbuilder}\index{projectbuilder@\texttt{projectbuilder}} layout, the installable tool whose folder plan Chapter~\ref{ch:workshop} first lays out by hand. Ingest the data with \texttt{webapi} (Chapter~\ref{ch:apis}) or \texttt{googlesheets} (Chapter~\ref{ch:sheets}). Render the online chart with \texttt{googlechart} and the offline county map with \texttt{sparkta2} into \texttt{charts/} (Chapter~\ref{ch:interactive}); build the drill-down dashboard with \texttt{statashiny}; then wrap narrative, dashboard, chart, and map into \texttt{index.html} with \texttt{webdoc2}, embedding the interactive pieces with \texttt{wdiframe} and the map with \texttt{wdimg}. Stamp the version and date into the footer at build time, tag the commit that built the folder, and name the folder to match (Section~\ref{sec:versioning}). The companion \texttt{ch12\_portal.do} sketches this exact sequence and scaffolds the stamped folder. The result is a folder the committee opens on any laptop, with no server and no logins, that the evaluator rebuilds next quarter in one command and that carries its own provenance so no copy is ever mistaken for another. The live example sites show the finished form.
\end{appliedexample}

\paragraph{Where this leaves you.} This chapter opened with an IT department blocking everything that needs a server and closes with the deliverable that does not care: a stamped folder of static files that rebuilds from one do-file, opens on any laptop, and answers for itself. Building it, you learned to compile the report from the analysis so the prose and the numbers cannot disagree, to add interactivity that carries its own engine, to pick the format by asking whether the reader will act inside the artifact or only read it, and to stamp every delivery with its version, commit, and data vintage so no one ever confuses which numbers a board saw. That stamped, rebuildable folder is the four principles in one object: replicable from a tagged commit, extensible by rerun, accessible behind any firewall, and actionable the day it lands. The shipping disciplines travel with it, a five-line release checklist with three sign-offs, a rebuild rehearsal that finds the broken link before the board does, and a scheduled build that leaves only the judgment to you. Part~V turns to sustaining the practice over time, beginning with the careful use of AI in Chapter~\ref{ch:ai}.

\section*{Exercises}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exercises}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Try it on your own data.} Take one dataset you already report on each quarter and write a short \texttt{webdoc2} do-file that prints one \texttt{stlog} table of an outcome by group, then confirm the number in your prose matches the number the log printed by reading them off the same page.
\item Rerun \texttt{ch12\_portal.do} against a second cause of death by changing only the \texttt{cause\_name} value in the stage-1 \texttt{webapi} call, and check that the online \texttt{googlechart} choropleth in \texttt{charts/} redraws with the new rates.
\item Build the three-move \texttt{statashiny} dashboard from the \texttt{auto} data, then add one \texttt{input(num)} slider that filters price, and confirm the table filters live when you drag the handle in the browser.
\item Extend the benchmark explorer of Section~\ref{sec:dashboardbuilder}: add the marriage rate as a fourth KPI tile in \texttt{ch12\_dashboardbuilder.do}, predict what the United States tile will read before you rebuild (the receipts habit), then rebuild and embed the refreshed page in a one-page \texttt{webdoc2} report with a single \texttt{wdiframe} line.
\item Predict how many rows the \texttt{collapse (mean) price mpg weight length (count) n=price, by(foreign)} step leaves, then run it and check that the row count matches your prediction before you feed it to \texttt{statashiny}.
\item Break the stamp on purpose: hard-code \enquote{v1.3} in the footer text but tag the built commit \texttt{v1.4}, then confirm that \texttt{git checkout v1.3} rebuilds a different folder than the footer claims, showing why the tag, not the typed string, is the promise worth keeping.
\item Stamp the data vintage: add one \texttt{webdoc put} line to your build that records both the short commit hash and the date you pulled the source data, then rebuild against a second extract of the same source and confirm the footer, not just the numbers, tells the two cuts apart.
\item Run the rebuild rehearsal on your own portal: copy the build to a fresh folder or clone the repository on a second machine, run the build do-file from the top, open \texttt{index.html} by double-click, and click every link and control once; record anything that rendered blank or failed to resolve in your release checklist, then fix the build rather than the copy.
\end{enumerate}

%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
% PART V
%----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

\addpart{Part V: Sustaining the Practice \newline Careful AI, Safe Sharing, and Shared Tools}

\chapter{Using AI without getting burned}\label{ch:ai}

Five thousand free-text case notes, one intern, and a coding deadline: this is the situation where a large language model looks like salvation, and where it most easily becomes a liability. Language models can code text, draft summaries, and scaffold analysis faster than any team could by hand, and they can also leak protected data, invent classifications, and produce a result that will not reproduce tomorrow. This chapter is the method and the guardrails that let an evaluator use them anyway.

We treat AI as a power tool that needs a jig, and this chapter builds the jig. First the method: use Stata as the backbone and the LLM as a coding partner rather than a replacement (Section~\ref{sec:backbone}). Then the guardrails that live inside that method: what must never leave the building, how to validate an LLM's output as a measurement rather than trust it as an oracle, how to make stochastic output reproducible, how to defend against untrusted text, and how to audit prediction for fairness when a predictive model helps decide who gets served. The chapter closes on the worked example that ties the guardrails together, because the guardrails only matter in combination.\marginnote{Most numbers in this chapter come from a small simulated dataset (seed \texttt{20260704}), built only so the checks run in front of you; the point is the procedure, not the figures, which would come from your own notes and your own LLM.}

\section{Stata as the backbone: the LLM as a coding partner}\label{sec:backbone}
The fastest way to get a wrong answer from an evaluation is to paste your data into a chat window and ask the LLM to \enquote{analyze it.} You get a fluent reply, a table, a paragraph of interpretation, and no way to tell what was actually computed, no way to rerun it next week, and no way to know it will say the same thing tomorrow. This section is the alternative, and it is the central method of the chapter: do not ask the LLM to \emph{be} your statistician. Make your statistical program the backbone, and let the LLM be the coding partner that drafts, tests, and extends the code the program runs. The LLM proposes; Stata disposes.

The reframe matters because the two tools have opposite strengths. A statistical program is stable, documented, versioned, and auditable, and it computes exactly what you tell it. A language model is fluent, fast, and creative, and it computes nothing at all. Put the LLM's fluency in front of the program's rigor, with the program always holding the results, and you get the speed of the one without surrendering the trustworthiness of the other. Put it the other way around, with the LLM holding the analysis, and you inherit every one of the failure modes below.

The partnership starts before the first prompt. Decompose the task into steps on paper, or directly in the checklist file of Step~1, before opening the assistant, because a step you cannot state crisply is a step no log can check. Five minutes of decomposition while calm settles questions that a deadline would otherwise settle badly: what the pieces are, what order they run in, and which pieces the LLM never touches at all.

\subsection{Why a language model is a poor statistical engine}\label{sec:llm-limits}
Four properties of large language models make them unfit to \emph{be} the analysis, and each one is a reason to place a stable program between the LLM and your results. The claims here are not folklore; each rests on published evidence cited in the bibliography.

\emph{Forgetting.} An LLM's attention to a long conversation is uneven: it uses information at the beginning and the end of its context window more reliably than information in the middle, a degradation \textcite{liu2024lost} document directly across many models and call \enquote{lost in the middle.}\index{lost in the middle}\marginnote{The curve is U-shaped: accuracy is highest when the needed fact sits at the beginning or end of the context and sags when it sits in the middle. A long analysis buries its early decisions exactly where the LLM attends least.} Over a multi-hour analysis, the LLM loses track of what it already ran, which variable it recoded, and why, and it will redo a step it finished an hour ago with a slightly different definition. The files must remember, because the LLM will not.

\emph{Drift.} An LLM's output is a probability distribution over text, so the same prompt can yield different code and different numbers on different runs; \textcite{ouyang2025nondeterminism} measure exactly this non-determinism in LLM code generation and show how much it undercuts reproducibility. Compounding it, the LLM behind a fixed API name is updated without notice: \textcite{chen2023drift} tracked two hosted LLMs on fixed tasks and found their accuracy on some tasks shifting sharply between the March and June 2023 versions carrying the same names, so an analysis that ran in January may behave differently in June for reasons you cannot see or log. A result you cannot reproduce is not a finding; it is a rumor.

\emph{No statistical engine.} A language model predicts the next token from patterns in its training text; it does not run least squares, invert a matrix, or compute a standard error. \textcite{bender2021parrots}, in the \enquote{stochastic parrots} critique, put the point sharply: the LLM captures the \emph{form} of language, not the meaning or the arithmetic behind it. It has read millions of regression tables and can produce something shaped like one, but asking it to \enquote{estimate} a coefficient in prose returns an imitation, not a calculation. The research on numerical tasks points the opposite way: accuracy rises sharply when the LLM is made to \emph{write code that a real interpreter executes} rather than compute in its own head. That is the finding behind program-aided language models\index{program-aided language models} \parencite{gao2023pal} and program-of-thoughts prompting \parencite{chen2023pot}, both of which delegate the computation to a Python interpreter and beat asking the LLM to reason numerically on its own.\marginnote{The machine-learning literature arrived at the section's thesis from its own direction: separate the reasoning (the LLM writes a program) from the computation (a real interpreter runs it). Stata is simply a better interpreter than Python for statistical work.} That is precisely the arrangement recommended here, with Stata as the interpreter: the missing statistical layer is not something to coax out of the LLM, it is the program and the data you already have.

\emph{Hallucination.} LLMs produce fluent, confident text that is sometimes simply false, a failure catalogued across generation tasks by \textcite{ji2023survey}. In generated code the problem is concrete: \textcite{spracklen2025packages} find that a large share of the packages LLMs recommend do not exist at all. In a statistical workflow this cuts two ways, and the two are not equally dangerous. A hallucinated command or option, an invented \texttt{regress} suboption that was never written, fails loudly the moment Stata runs it, which is a gift. A hallucinated \emph{number} in a prose summary, a coefficient or a sample size the LLM made up, fails silently and can travel all the way into a funder's report. The defense against both is the same: never let a number reach the page unless a program produced it and a log records it.

None of this is an argument against using language models; it is an argument about where to stand them. Their weaknesses, forgetting, drift, the absence of real computation, and hallucination, are exactly the weaknesses a stable, documented, version-controlled program does not have. Surveying the same trade-off for economists, \textcite{korinek2023genai} reaches the matching division of labor: the LLM accelerates the ideation, writing, and coding around research while the verification of anything that matters stays with the researcher and the tools that actually compute. The method below is that division made operational for an evaluation shop: the program in the load-bearing position, the LLM where it is strong, writing and revising code one checkable piece at a time.

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=0.45cm and 0.9cm]
  \node[substep, text width=2.0cm] (todo) {\textbf{Checklist}\\\texttt{TODO.md}\\(external memory)};
  \node[stage, right=of todo] (llm) {LLM drafts \emph{one} step};
  \node[stage, right=of llm] (run) {Stata in batch\\\texttt{stata -b do}};
  \node[stage, right=of run] (log) {Log $+$ \texttt{assert}\\(ground truth)};
  \node[substep, right=of log, text width=2.0cm] (tri) {Triangulate\\(2nd method,\\adversarial check)};
  \draw[flow] (todo) -- (llm);
  \draw[flow] (llm) -- (run);
  \draw[flow] (run) -- (log);
  \draw[flow] (log) -- node[above, font=\sffamily\scriptsize] {pass} (tri);
  \draw[flow] (log) to[out=110, in=70]
      node[above, font=\sffamily\scriptsize] {fail: hand the log back} (llm);
  \draw[flow] (tri) to[out=-90, in=-90]
      node[below, font=\sffamily\scriptsize]
      {tick the item, update the docs, next step} (todo);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[The backbone loop]{The backbone loop. An external checklist holds the plan; the LLM drafts one step at a time; Stata runs it in batch and the log, with \texttt{assert} tripwires, is the only thing trusted to say the step worked; a failing step hands the log back to the LLM to fix; a passing step is triangulated against a second method, then the checklist and the running documentation are updated before the next step. The LLM never holds the results; the files do, so you can step in at any point.}
\label{fig:backbone}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{The method in one loop: propose, run, verify, log}
The method is the loop in Figure~\ref{fig:backbone}, and four rules discipline it. The LLM works one step at a time, drafting or extending a single do-file rather than the whole analysis, so every change is small enough to check. Every step runs in batch Stata and is judged by its log, never by the LLM's assurance that the code is correct. Every passing step is triangulated against a second method or an adversarial check before it is trusted. And the LLM writes down what it did, in the checklist and the running documentation, so the project's memory lives in files rather than in a context window that will forget.

Working this way costs discipline, so it had better pay, and it pays four ways. The workflow is reproducible, because it is a sequence of logged do-files, not a conversation. You can step in at any point, because the state of the project is on disk, not in the LLM's head, so you are never held hostage by an LLM that has forgotten what it did. It is more stable, because Stata's behavior is pinned by \texttt{version} while the LLM's is not. And it is extensible: a vetted pipeline becomes the starting point for the next project, which saves both effort and the tokens you would spend re-deriving it. This is the same reproducibility discipline that \textcite{long2009workflow} made the foundation of credible quantitative work in his \emph{Workflow of Data Analysis Using Stata}, now extended to a collaborator that happens not to be human.\marginnote{Long's book predates the LLM entirely, which is the point: nothing about the discipline changes when the code's first draft comes from an LLM. Scripted, documented, logged, replicable, the standards are the same, and the LLM must meet them.}

\subsection{Step 1: give the LLM an external checklist it must keep}
The first artifact is not code; it is a plain-text checklist that lives outside the LLM. Because the LLM forgets the middle of a long exchange (Section~\ref{sec:llm-limits}) and because a real prompt carries many requirements, the single highest-leverage habit is to write the plan down in a Markdown file and require the LLM to update it after every step, recording what it did, or that it skipped an item and why. This one move stops the silent omissions that are otherwise almost guaranteed on a long task.

\begin{kaobox}[frametitle=The checklist the LLM maintains: \texttt{TODO.md}]
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
# Analysis checklist  (LLM updates after every step)
- [x] 100_ingest   loaded 2,246 rows from nlsw88     DONE
- [x] 120_clean    lowpay = wage<5; asserted N=757   DONE
- [ ] 200_model    wage ~ educ + tenure, cluster ind PENDING
- [~] 210_robust   SKIPPED for now: needs a decision
                   on whether to log-transform wage
\end{verbatim}
}
Instruct the LLM, in the system prompt, the standing instructions an assistant reads before anything you type, to treat this file as the source of truth: read it first, do the next unchecked item, then write back what it did before stopping. The \texttt{[\textasciitilde]} marker for a deliberately skipped item, with the reason, keeps a caveat from silently vanishing.
\end{kaobox}

A checklist is only as checkable as its steps, and there is a craft to writing them. The working rule is one action, one observable check, one file touched: \texttt{120\_clean} recodes one indicator, asserts one count, and writes one log, so the item can be ticked or left unticked with no judgment call. A step written as \enquote{clean the data} fails the rule three ways at once, and the failure is not cosmetic; a step with no single observable check is a step the LLM can declare finished without being wrong in any way a log could catch.\marginnote{When a draft step resists the one-action form, that resistance is information: the step is really two steps, and splitting it now is cheaper than untangling it after a tripwire fires somewhere in the middle.}

The same planning pass is the moment to draw the data-touching boundary: which steps the LLM may draft (recodes, merges, chart code, robustness variants) and which stay human regardless of the deadline (the gold-standard hand-coding of Section~\ref{sec:aiverify}, the sign-off on any number that ships, anything that moves identified records). Marking each item \texttt{llm} or \texttt{human} costs one column in the checklist and settles, in writing and in advance, the question a deadline answers worst.

\subsection{Step 2: scaffold the project and its documentation layer}
The second step hands the LLM a workspace that documents itself. The \texttt{projectbuilder}\index{projectbuilder@\texttt{projectbuilder}} scaffold of Chapter~\ref{ch:workshop} lays down the folders and the control file; the addition here is a documentation layer the LLM writes \emph{as it works}, so the record is a by-product of the analysis rather than a chore at the end. As the LLM cleans and explores, it appends to a running \texttt{webdoc2}\index{webdoc2@\texttt{webdoc2}} report (Chapter~\ref{ch:portals}), regenerates a variable-level codebook, and tags each variable's origin with \texttt{srctag}\index{srctag@\texttt{srctag}} (Chapter~\ref{ch:panels}). The result is the initial pass a careful research assistant would make before the real work begins: distributions, missingness, outliers, and provenance, all written down.

\begin{kaobox}[frametitle=Package Integration: \texttt{datadictionary} as the living codebook]
\textbf{Integrated command:} \texttt{datadictionary}\index{datadictionary@\texttt{datadictionary}} (Chapter~\ref{ch:surveys}) is the codebook layer of this scaffold. Have the LLM's control file invoke it after every data step, one in-memory call, \texttt{datadictionary, excel(codebook.xlsx) replace}, and the workspace keeps a current dictionary of variable types, value labels, missingness rates, example values, and each variable's \texttt{srctag} origin, in a workbook the \texttt{webdoc2} report embeds. The documentation then tracks the data's actual state at each step, so a reviewer can see exactly what the LLM encountered and what it changed, rather than trusting a summary written from memory. Add \texttt{saving()} to each call and the codebook is also a dataset, so the control file can diff this step's codebook against the last one and halt the loop when the data shifted in a way the checklist never planned.
\end{kaobox}

One more file belongs in the scaffold while it is still empty: the prompt-and-version log of Section~\ref{ch13-sc-promptlog}, which costs a header row on day one and an afternoon of forensics if it is started after the fact.

\subsection{Step 3: the propose, run, verify loop with tripwires}
This is the heart of the method. The LLM drafts one do-file; you run it in batch Stata from the shell; the log decides whether it worked. The move that makes the log trustworthy is to fill the do-file with \emph{tripwires}, \texttt{assert}\index{assert@\texttt{assert}} statements that stop the run the instant reality departs from the LLM's claim about it. An assertion is a one-line contract: state what must be true, and if it is not, Stata halts with an error rather than sailing on with wrong data.

Here a step recodes a low-pay indicator, and the assertions pin down every property that must hold: no missing values introduced, strictly binary, one row per worker preserved, and an expected count the checklist justifies.\marginnote{The drill runs on the familiar NLSW extract on purpose: with data this boring and this well known since Chapter~\ref{ch:embedded}, every surprise in a run belongs to the pipeline, not the data.}
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* 120_clean.do
sysuse nlsw88, clear
gen byte lowpay = wage < 5      // the LLM's proposed step
assert !missing(lowpay)         // no silent missings
assert inlist(lowpay, 0, 1)     // strictly binary
isid idcode                     // still one row per worker
count if lowpay
assert r(N) == 757      // expected count, per checklist
di as result "STEP 120 PASSED"
\end{verbatim}
}
Run it from the shell, not from inside the chat, so the run is real and logged. The \texttt{-b} flag is Stata's batch mode: it runs the do-file top to bottom with no interface and writes the log beside it\marginnote{Swap \texttt{stata-mp} for whichever executable your license installs; the \texttt{-b} flag behaves the same way in each.}:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
stata-mp -b do 120_clean.do      // writes 120_clean.log
\end{verbatim}
}
In the first runs of this step, the expected count was guessed wrong twice, once by the LLM and once by the human checking it; Stata answered both guesses the same way:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
. assert r(N) == 132
assertion is false
r(9);
\end{verbatim}
}
That failure is the method working. The wrong number never reached a table; it hit a tripwire, the log recorded it, and the LLM was handed the log to fix, which it did by reading the true count (757) rather than by insisting. When the contract finally held, the log said so plainly, and only then did the step count as done. The rule is simple and absolute: a step is finished when the log proves it, not when the LLM believes it.

Two routing rules keep this loop honest, and both are worth fixing before the first run so they operate as reflexes rather than judgment calls. When a tripwire fires, the LLM gets the log itself, pasted verbatim, never a paraphrase of it: the return code, the failing line, and the true count are the diagnostic, and a summary written from memory strips exactly the details the LLM needs while adding a human's guess about the cause, which the LLM will obligingly pursue instead of the evidence. And a step earns its tick only after two clean passes: rerun the passing do-file once more from a fresh batch instance, and proceed when the second log matches the first. A step that passes once may have leaned on leftover state in memory; a step that passes twice from scratch is reproducible by construction, and the second run costs seconds.

\subsection{Step 4: triangulate, and try to break the finding}
A passing test proves the code ran, not that the answer is right, so in the last step of each loop you reach the number a second way. Re-estimate a key coefficient with a different command or a different subsample and confirm it lands in the same place; hand the finding to a second LLM or a fresh agent whose only instruction is to refute it; spot-check one computed value by hand against the raw data.\marginnote{This is the same posture as the sensitivity analysis of Chapter~\ref{ch:claims} and the gold-standard validation of Section~\ref{sec:aiverify}, now applied to the whole workflow rather than a single estimate.} Decide the fork before the comparison runs: name the tolerance within which the two numbers count as agreeing, proceed and record both when they do, and when they do not, make the discrepancy its own checklist item that blocks everything downstream until it is resolved. A tolerance chosen after seeing the gap is a tolerance chosen to close it. A finding that survives an honest attempt to break it is one you can defend; a finding that has only ever been agreed with is not.

\subsection{Going parallel: two worked patterns}\label{sec:backbone-parallel}
Two bottlenecks in this loop are worth parallelizing, and both fit the backbone philosophy exactly, because each parallel worker is still a logged, reproducible batch job. The first speeds up slow estimation; the second speeds up review.

\paragraph{Pattern 1: split a slow estimation across instances.} Some estimates are slow by nature: a multilevel model on millions of rows, a bootstrap with thousands of replicates, a specification curve of hundreds of fits. When the pieces are independent, the work is \emph{embarrassingly parallel}\index{embarrassingly parallel}: split it into separate batch jobs, run them on several Stata instances (or several machines) at once, and combine the saved results. Here a bootstrap of a wage regression's tenure coefficient is split four ways. The worker takes a tag, a replicate count, and a seed, and posts its replicate estimates to its own file:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* boot_worker.do        (args: tag reps seed)
args tag reps seed
set seed `seed'
sysuse nlsw88, clear
postfile buf double b using "boot_`tag'.dta", replace
forvalues r = 1/`reps' {
    preserve
        bsample
        quietly regress wage tenure grade age
        post buf (_b[tenure])
    restore
}
postclose buf
\end{verbatim}
}
\marginnote{The worker rests on three commands: \texttt{postfile} opens a results buffer that writes one row per \texttt{post}, \texttt{bsample} redraws the sample with replacement (the bootstrap's core move), and \texttt{postclose} seals the file for the harvest step.}An orchestrator (a shell loop, or an agent) launches four instances at once, each with its own seed; the trailing \texttt{\&} pushes each run to the background so they overlap, and \texttt{wait} holds the script until all four finish:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
for t in 1 2 3 4; do
  stata-mp -b do boot_worker.do $t 250 $((1000+t)) &
done; wait
\end{verbatim}
}
\marginnote{On an eight-core laptop the four jobs ran together in under two seconds of wall time; the \texttt{\&} backgrounds each job and \texttt{wait} blocks until all four finish.}A short combine step stacks the four files and reads off the bootstrap standard error and interval:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
clear
forvalues t = 1/4 {
    append using "boot_`t'.dta"
}
summarize b
local se = r(sd)
_pctile b, p(2.5 97.5)
di "reps=" _N "  SE=" %5.3f `se' ///
   "  95%CI=[" %5.3f r(r1) "," %5.3f r(r2) "]"
\end{verbatim}
}
The real run returns a thousand-replicate bootstrap with a standard error of \texttt{0.019} and a 95\% interval of \texttt{[0.112, 0.187]}, computed in the wall-clock time of a two-hundred-fifty-replicate one, with every job a do-file you can rerun. Push the same split across machines and the arithmetic scales further; the only rule is that each worker seeds differently so the replicates are independent. Sketch the split in the checklist before launching anything: the number of workers, each worker's seed, and the replicate total the combine step will assert against, so the parallel run is a plan you wrote rather than a pattern the LLM improvised at the shell.

\paragraph{Pattern 2: fan out the code review.} On a large pipeline a single review pass is both a bottleneck and a blind spot. Split the codebase into slices, hand each to its own reviewer (an agent, or just its own batch run), and give each the same narrow brief: find the bug, the unhandled missing, the merge that silently drops rows. Independent reviewers in parallel catch what one sequential pass misses, and because each reads only its slice, none is defeated by a context window too small for the whole project. The mechanical half of this is a harness that runs every step in its own instance at once and then judges each by its log:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
for f in *.do; do
  stata-mp -b do "$f" >/dev/null 2>&1 &
done; wait
for f in *.do; do
  log=${f%.do}.log
  grep -qE '^r\([0-9]+\);' "$log" \
     && echo "FAIL $f" || echo "pass $f"
done
\end{verbatim}
}
One detail in that harness is a genuine trap, and it is the reason the check reads the log rather than the exit code.

\begin{vizcallout}[The batch-exit-code trap]
Stata in batch mode (\texttt{-b}) writes any error to the log but still exits with shell status \texttt{0}. A harness that trusts \texttt{\$?} will call every step a pass, including the ones that failed. Judge each run by grepping its log for an error line (\texttt{r(\#);}), not by the exit code. In the run above, that grep correctly flagged the one seeded-in bug, \texttt{FAIL 200\_clean.do (r(9))}, while the two clean steps passed.
\end{vizcallout}

The agent version of this pattern replaces the \texttt{grep} with a reviewer LLM per slice, each returning the issues it found; you deduplicate the reports and feed only the real ones back into the loop of Figure~\ref{fig:backbone}. Either way the principle holds: parallelize the work, but keep every piece a logged, checkable artifact.

\begin{appliedexample}[A one-hour reanalysis that survives the LLM changing]\label{ae:ch13-reanalysis}
\textbf{Scenario.} A funder asks, on short notice, whether a wage gap in last quarter's analysis holds up. The original was run against an LLM version that has since been updated.

\medskip
\textbf{The backbone run.} You do not reopen the chat and ask again, because that LLM is gone. You open the project folder. The \texttt{TODO.md} shows every step and its status; the numbered do-files rerun from raw data with one command; the \texttt{webdoc2} report shows what the assistant found and decided last time; the logs show every \texttt{assert} that passed. You point a fresh LLM at the checklist, ask it to add one robustness check, run it in batch, triangulate the new number against the old log, and answer the funder in an hour, with a result that does not depend on any LLM remembering anything. The LLM changed; the analysis did not, because the LLM was never the analysis.
\end{appliedexample}

\paragraph{Where this leaves you.} You can now use a language model the way it pays off and none of the ways it burns you: as a fast coding partner working one checkable step at a time, with Stata holding the results, an external checklist holding the plan, batch logs and \texttt{assert} tripwires holding the truth, and triangulation guarding the finding. This is the constructive half of the chapter; the guardrails that follow, privacy, validation, reproducibility, injection, and fairness, are the specific safety mechanisms that live inside this loop.

\section{What never leaves the building}
Before an evaluator hands anything to a hosted AI, the question to settle is not what the tool can do but what data it may see. Administrative records are often protected by FERPA, HIPAA, or a data-use agreement that forbids sending them to an outside server, and one leaked record can end the data-sharing relationship the whole evaluation depends on. So we strip direct and indirect identifiers from text before any API call, we read the terms of service to confirm the vendor does not retain or train on the data, and for records that legally cannot leave the building we run a local, open-source LLM (Ollama, \texttt{llama.cpp}) on the agency's own hardware.\marginnote{The line to watch in a vendor's terms is the retention window and the training opt-out, which are separate promises: \enquote{we do not train on your data} says nothing about how long a copy sits on their servers or which subprocessors can read it.}

\begin{kaobox}[frametitle=Package Integration: \texttt{ai\_privacy\_gate}]
\textbf{Integrated command:} \texttt{ai\_privacy\_gate}\index{ai\_privacy\_gate@\texttt{ai\_privacy\_gate}}. Scans string variables for six likely-PII pattern classes (Social Security numbers, phone numbers, email addresses, birth-format dates, street addresses, and tagged record numbers), reports what it found by class, and in its \texttt{action(stop)} mode halts the do-file when anything would leak. Install from the authors' GitHub; Section~\ref{sec:ch13-aigate} runs it as a pipeline gate.
\end{kaobox}

The boundary works best decided during the task decomposition of Section~\ref{sec:backbone}, not negotiated field by field once the pipeline is running. While writing the checklist, mark each step hosted-eligible or local-only, and next to each local-only step name the field that makes it so (the case narrative with names in it, the date of birth, the address). The intern who inherits the pipeline then reads the boundary instead of guessing it, and the data-use agreement has a written counterpart the agency can inspect.

Privacy is not one concern among several here; it is the precondition for using these tools at all.\marginnote{The failure is rarely a malicious breach; it is a well-meaning analyst pasting a spreadsheet into a chat window to \enquote{just clean it up quickly.} A gate that runs by default beats a policy that depends on everyone remembering the policy under deadline.} An evaluator who cannot guarantee where the data goes cannot responsibly use a hosted LLM, which is why the gate comes first, before any question of accuracy.

\subsection{A gate the pipeline cannot skip}\label{sec:ch13-aigate}
The tripwires of Section~\ref{sec:backbone} rest on one idea: a claim about the data is worth a line of code that halts the run the moment the claim fails. Privacy deserves the same enforcement, and needs it more, because the failure is worse in one specific way. An \texttt{assert} that fires late costs you a rerun; a case note that leaves for a hosted API is simply gone, beyond recall, whatever the vendor's retention policy says. The boundary you marked on the checklist, hosted-eligible or local-only, is a policy, and a policy depends on everyone remembering it under deadline. \texttt{ai\_privacy\_gate} turns the policy into a tripwire: put it at the top of any do-file that calls an API, give it \texttt{action(stop)}, and the pipeline refuses to run while anything that looks like an identifier is still in the text. The data-use agreement's sentence \enquote{no identified records leave the building} now has a line of code that enforces it, which is a different thing from a reminder that recommends it.

Figure~\ref{fig:ch13-aigate} is the whole arrangement. The gate sits between the notes and the API call. Clean text passes. Flagged text stops the run, and the recovery path is deliberate: mask a \emph{copy}, prove the mask held by running the gate again, and send only the redacted copy out, while the unmasked originals never leave the machine.

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\resizebox{0.98\linewidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=0.55cm and 1.3cm]
  \node[stage] (notes) {Case\\notes};
  \node[stage, right=of notes] (gate) {\texttt{ai\_privacy\_gate}\\\texttt{action(stop)}};
  \node[stage, above right=0.45cm and 1.3cm of gate] (api) {Hosted\\LLM};
  \node[stage, below right=0.45cm and 1.3cm of gate] (mask) {Mask a\\copy};
  \draw[flow] (notes) -- (gate);
  \draw[flow] (gate) -- node[annot, above, sloped] {clean: passes} (api);
  \draw[flow] (gate) -- node[annot, below, sloped] {flagged: halts} (mask);
  \draw[flow] (mask) -- node[annot, right=2pt] {re-gate, then send the copy} (api);
  \node[substep, below=0.55cm of notes, text width=2.6cm] {Unmasked originals stay local, always};
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption[The privacy gate in a coding pipeline]{The privacy gate in a coding pipeline. \texttt{ai\_privacy\_gate} with \texttt{action(stop)} halts the do-file the moment any note matches a PII pattern, so text reaches the hosted LLM only by passing the gate. The recovery path masks a copy, proves the mask held by running the gate on the copy again, and sends only the redacted version; the unmasked originals never leave the machine. Built by \texttt{ch13\_validation.do}.}
\label{fig:ch13-aigate}
\end{figure*}

Watch it work on a dozen simulated case notes, three lines of them deliberately seeded with the leaks a real batch contains\marginnote{The seeded leaks: a Social Security number recorded at intake, callback phone numbers, an email address, a birth date, a street address, and two tagged record numbers.} (part (f) of \texttt{ch13\_validation.do} builds the file):
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
. ai_privacy_gate notes comments, genflag(pii)

AI privacy gate: 2 text variable(s) scanned, action = report
----------------------------------------------------------
class         flagged rows
----------------------------------------------------------
ssn                      1
phone                    2
email                    2
date                     1
address                  1
idnum                    2
----------------------------------------------------------
any class                9
\end{verbatim}
}
Nine of the twelve notes carry something the gate refuses to pass\marginnote{The realistic surprise: caseworkers write phone numbers into narratives, because narratives are where the work happens.}. The \texttt{genflag()} column routes those nine to local-only handling. For the batch that must go out, the recovery path is three lines, and the third is the proof:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
preserve
ai_privacy_gate notes comments, action(mask)
* the re-run passes only if the mask held:
ai_privacy_gate notes comments, action(stop)
\end{verbatim}
}
The second gate run is the same move as re-running a test battery after a fix: the mask is not trusted because the command printed a reassuring message, it is trusted because the gate that would have halted the pipeline no longer does.\marginnote{A pattern scan is a floor, not a guarantee. Free text can identify a person with no pattern at all: \enquote{the only welder in Marfa} names someone as surely as an SSN. The gate catches the leaks that look like data; the human review of Section~\ref{sec:backbone}'s local-only column still owns the judgment.}

\section{A measurement, not an oracle}\label{sec:aiverify}
Can an LLM actually code text as well as the humans an evaluator would otherwise hire? The published benchmarks say often yes, and that is precisely why the discipline in this section matters. \textcite{gilardi2023annotation} found that a general-purpose LLM, with no task-specific training, beat crowd workers on several political-text annotation tasks at a fraction of the cost, and \textcite{ziems2024css} tested LLMs across two dozen computational-social-science tasks and reached the more useful conclusion for practice: performance is real but uneven, strong on some constructs and mediocre on others, with no way to know which in advance except to check. Uneven-but-often-good is exactly the profile of a measurement instrument, not an oracle, so we treat every LLM classification as a measurement that must be validated on your task, against your coders, before it is trusted. The protocol is the one an evaluator already knows from inter-rater reliability\index{inter-rater reliability}: hand-code a random gold-standard subset of 100 to 200 records, compare the LLM's labels against it, and require an agreement threshold (Cohen's or Fleiss' kappa\index{kappa!Cohen's and Fleiss'}) before scaling to the full dataset.\sidenote{Cohen's kappa is for exactly two raters; Fleiss' generalizes to three or more. If you validate the LLM against a single human coder, Cohen's is the right statistic; if you pool several coders' labels or compare several LLMs, reach for Fleiss'.} Where several LLMs or prompts are run, their consensus and disagreement are themselves informative, and low-agreement cases are exactly the ones to route to a human.

Raw agreement overstates how well an LLM is doing, because two raters will match on some records by luck alone. Kappa\index{kappa!formula} is the fix: it subtracts the agreement you would expect by chance and reports what is left,
\[
\kappa \;=\; \frac{p_o - p_e}{1 - p_e},
\]
where $p_o$ is the observed share of records the LLM and the human labelled the same, and $p_e$ is the share they would match on by chance alone given how often each category is used. The denominator is the room left above chance, so $\kappa=1$ is perfect agreement and $\kappa=0$ is no better than luck. To see it work, we take a simulated gold standard of 150 case notes hand-coded into five barrier categories, run an LLM that copies the human label 85\% of the time, and compare the two with \texttt{kap}\index{kap@\texttt{kap}}:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
* truth = human gold standard; model = ~85% agreement
kap truth model
\end{verbatim}
}
The output separates raw agreement from chance and reports the corrected statistic:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
             Expected
Agreement   agreement    Kappa   Std. err.      Z   Prob>Z
----------------------------------------------------------
  86.00%      20.40%    0.8241     0.0411   20.05   0.0000
\end{verbatim}
}
Read this in the units of the decision, not the abstract. The LLM matched the human coder on 86\% of the 150 notes, but with five roughly balanced categories about 20\% of that agreement is what chance alone would deliver. Kappa strips the luck out and leaves 0.82, comfortably above the 0.75 line most evaluators set before scaling.\marginnote{Landis and Koch's rough bands: 0.41--0.60 moderate, 0.61--0.80 substantial, above 0.80 almost perfect. Treat them as signposts, not law; a kappa of 0.82 on a five-way task is a green light, but still read the confusion matrix to see which category the LLM confuses.} The $Z$ of 20 and $p<0.001$ only say the agreement is not zero, which is a low bar; the number that licenses scaling is the 0.82 itself, not its significance. As a check on the simulation itself: the LLM here was built to copy the human 85\% of the time and to pick a different category otherwise, so with five near-even categories the expected kappa is $(0.85-0.20)/(1-0.20)=0.81$, and the 0.82 that prints is that arithmetic plus sampling noise.

Set the fork before the kappa prints. Above the threshold named in the plan (0.75 here), scale to the full dataset and carry the kappa into the write-up; below it, the useful question is which failure the confusion matrix shows. Errors concentrated in one category point to a prompt revision, or to a category definition the human coders themselves would argue about; errors spread evenly point to a task the LLM cannot yet do, where the honest next step is more hand-coding rather than a lower threshold. Fix the forks in advance, and a near-miss of 0.73 cannot be rounded up by the wish to be done.

\begin{kaobox}[frametitle=Package Integration: \texttt{llmsieve}]
\textbf{Integrated command:} \texttt{llmsieve}\index{llmsieve@\texttt{llmsieve}}. Computes this section's convergence delta for every row, one Mata Levenshtein pass per pair, flags the rows whose delta clears a threshold, and hands back the routing variables (\texttt{gendelta()}, \texttt{genflag()}), so the human-review budget goes exactly where the LLMs disagree. It measures stability, not truth: the gold-standard kappa of Section~\ref{sec:aiverify} stays in the design. Ships in the companion repository's \texttt{code/} folder.
\end{kaobox}

Watch it on four two-pass answers, two identical, one trivially reworded, one genuinely rewritten (part (g) of \texttt{ch13\_validation.do}):
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
. llmsieve pass1 pass2, gendelta(delta) genflag(review)

llmsieve: convergence delta between pass1 and pass2 (N = 4)
  mean delta  0.206, max  0.786
  rows above threshold(0.15), routed to human review: 1 of 4

  pass1                            delta   review
  transportation barrier           0.000        0
  childcare gap on Tuesdays        0.040        0
  no barrier reported              0.000        0
  schedule conflict with work      0.786        1
\end{verbatim}
}
The trivial rewording, Tuesdays to Tuesday, scores 0.04 and passes; the answer that changed its mind scores 0.786 and goes to a human. That is the routing this section promised, now one line long.

\subsection{The weakest category is the one to spot-check}\label{sec:kappa-by-category}
A single kappa hides where the LLM struggles, so the next question is which categories it gets wrong. Charting agreement one barrier at a time turns the scalar into a map (Figure~\ref{fig:agreement}): four categories sit near 0.85, but \enquote{none} lands lowest at 0.81, which is the category to spot-check and the one \texttt{llmsieve} would route to a human first.

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
graph bar (mean) agree, ///
    over(truth, label(labsize(small))) ///
    blabel(bar, format(%4.2f))
\end{verbatim}
}
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{ch13_agreement.png}
\caption[Model-human agreement by category]{Share of notes on which the simulated LLM matches the hand-coded gold standard, one bar per barrier category (150 simulated notes, seed \texttt{20260704}). Overall agreement is high, but the \enquote{none} category is weakest at 0.81, which is exactly where you spot-check the LLM and where a consensus tool routes cases to a human. A single kappa would have hidden this.}
\label{fig:agreement}
\end{figure*}

A reviewer cannot weigh \enquote{the LLM said so.} A reviewer can weigh a measured error rate, and validation is the step that produces one, which is why a validated AI-coded variable is publishable and an unvalidated one is not. Treating the LLM as a measurement rather than an oracle is the single habit that separates responsible use from wishful use.

\subsection{Consensus across models}\label{sec:consensus}
One LLM gives you a label; several LLMs give you a confidence signal for free. When the same 150 notes are coded by three different LLMs, the number that agree on the modal label tells you which cases are safe to trust and which need a human, without any extra hand-coding. We run two more simulated LLMs alongside the first\marginnote{The two extra simulated LLMs agree with the truth about 80\% and 78\% of the time, against the first LLM's 85\%.} and count, per note, how many of the three back the most common label. Tabulating that count gives a triage table:

{\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
\hline
Consensus & Notes & Share & Action \\
\hline
3/3 unanimous  &  80 & 53.3\% & auto-accept \\
2/3 majority   &  58 & 38.7\% & accept, spot-check \\
1/3 split      &  12 &  8.0\% & route to human \\
\hline
Total          & 150 & 100.0\% & \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
}

\medskip
Read the table as a workload plan. On this simulated set the three LLMs agree unanimously on 53\% of notes, which you can accept with the lightest touch, and they split three ways on only 8\%, which is a manageable stack of twelve notes for a human to resolve rather than 150.\marginnote{Consensus is a proxy for confidence, not a substitute for the gold standard. Three LLMs can agree and all be wrong the same way, especially on an ambiguous category. Keep validating against hand labels; use consensus to decide where to spend the human review budget, not whether to spend it.} The value is that the human effort goes exactly where the LLMs are least sure, so a fixed review budget buys the most error reduction. Coders that are each mostly right, and at least somewhat independent, will usually converge, so the cases where they scatter are the genuinely hard ones. Disagreement is not noise to suppress; it is the pipeline telling you where its own error lives.

\subsection{The sieve: turning disagreement into a black-box trust proxy}\label{sec:sieve}
The consensus table works because you had three labels to count, but a hosted LLM hands you only its answer, never the per-sentence probability behind it, so you cannot ask it how sure it is. This subsection is the workaround that the \texttt{llmsieve} box above rests on: when the LLM exposes no internal uncertainty, measure uncertainty from its \emph{behavior} instead. Send one LLM's output to a second LLM for refinement, then measure how much the second changed the first. If successive passes barely move the text, the region is stable; if they rewrite it, the LLMs are unsure, and that case is the one to route to a human. The reasoning inside \texttt{llmsieve} is exactly this: turn silent internal uncertainty into a visible edit.

The framing that makes this rigorous is to treat each LLM as a noisy sensor, an imperfect instrument pointed at a truth you cannot read directly, the way an evaluator already treats two hand-coders of the same case note.\marginnote{This is the same logic as inter-rater reliability, one level up: instead of asking whether two humans agree on a label, you ask whether two LLMs agree on the \emph{wording} of an answer. Agreement in wording is a cheap proxy for the confidence the hosted LLM will not give you.} A single sensor reading tells you little about its own error; two readings that land in the same place suggest the reading is precise, and two that scatter suggest it is not. Semantic-uncertainty research makes the same move from the inside of open-weight models: \textcite{kuhn2023semantic} cluster an LLM's own resampled answers by meaning and read uncertainty off how much they scatter, and \textcite{farquhar2024semantic}, in \emph{Nature}, show that this semantic entropy detects hallucinations across LLMs and tasks. The sieve is the black-box cousin of that idea. Where semantic entropy needs the LLM's sampled distribution, the sieve needs only two finished strings, which is all a hosted API returns, so it works on exactly the closed LLMs an evaluator most often has to use.

The measurement is a normalized edit distance between the two strings, and we call it the convergence delta. Take the Levenshtein distance, the number of single-character insertions, deletions, or substitutions that turn one answer into the other, and divide by the length of the longer answer so the result sits between zero and one regardless of how long the answers are. Written out, the convergence delta\index{convergence delta} between the two answers $R_1$ and $R_2$ is
\[
\Delta(R_1,R_2)
\;=\;
\frac{\operatorname{Lev}(R_1,R_2)}{\max\!\bigl(|R_1|,\,|R_2|\bigr)}
\in [0,1],
\qquad
\text{route} =
\begin{cases}
\text{accept} & \Delta < \tau,\\[2pt]
\text{human}  & \Delta \ge \tau,
\end{cases}
\]
where $\operatorname{Lev}$ is the Levenshtein distance (single-character edits from one string to the other), $|R|$ is a string's character length, and $\tau$ is the routing threshold set below to $0.10$. A delta near zero means the second LLM left the first almost untouched, the stable region; a large delta means it rewrote the answer, the region of epistemic uncertainty. The whole computation is small enough to write in Mata, Stata's compiled matrix language\marginnote{Type \texttt{mata:} to enter Mata and \texttt{end} to leave; its loops and typed functions read like stripped-down C.}, and read in one screen, so the trust proxy is a function you can inspect line by line rather than a remote service you cannot see inside:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
mata:
real scalar lev(string scalar a,
                string scalar b) {
    real scalar na, nb, i, j, c
    real matrix d
    na = strlen(a) ; nb = strlen(b)
    if (na==0) return(nb)
    if (nb==0) return(na)
    d = J(na+1, nb+1, 0)
    for (i=1; i<=na+1; i++) d[i,1] = i-1
    for (j=1; j<=nb+1; j++) d[1,j] = j-1
    for (i=2; i<=na+1; i++) {
      for (j=2; j<=nb+1; j++) {
        c = (substr(a,i-1,1)==
             substr(b,j-1,1) ? 0 : 1)
        d[i,j] = min((d[i-1,j]+1, d[i,j-1]+1,
                      d[i-1,j-1]+c))
      }
    }
    return(d[na+1,nb+1])
}
real scalar delta(string scalar a,
                  string scalar b) {
    real scalar m
    m = max((strlen(a), strlen(b)))
    if (m==0) return(0)
    return(lev(a,b)/m)
}
end
\end{verbatim}
}
With the function defined, we feed it two pairs of simulated LLM answers: one stable pair, where a second LLM only tidied punctuation, and one divergent pair, where two LLMs disagreed on the barrier category outright. Thresholding the delta at \texttt{0.10} turns each into a routing decision:
\par
{\footnotesize
\begin{verbatim}
mata:
s1 = "Barrier: transportation, no bus route"
s2 = "Barrier: transportation; no bus route."
